It will be improved by removing the domestic activities and increasing the importance of the medical subjects. ParlaMint-AT_2016-06-16-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00134_d7e7135	Thank you, Madam President. Mrs. State Secretary! Ladies and gentlemen! Signature and trust service law, of course, already sounds somewhat restricted. It is about the renewal of an existing law, namely the Signature Act, and the conversion of an EU regulation, which is in itself directly applicable, into a legislative text to: This is the first time that the European Parliament has been able to give its opinion on the proposal for a Directive on the approximation of the laws, regulations and administrative provisions of the Member States on the approximation of the This makes sense, because there was simply some terminology in this EU regulation, because other formulations are used there. In this respect, it was useful to implement this and to pour it into an Austrian law. But if you did something like this, I would have expected more clarity and accuracy here and hoped that you would make things better on this occasion. It is welcome that digitalisation is progressing. This so-called qualified electronic signature is about reproducing the written text in the electronic field and making rules here, which means writtenness when you are in electronic commerce. This is increasing more and more, that is not a question, is an important area. On the Internet, more and more is being done, and it is therefore important to regulate this. But it is of course equally important or almost even more important that you create the possibilities here on the one hand and also increase the applicability of putting security in the foreground on the other hand, because you know exactly how much happens and how much fraud there is and so on. Therefore, I would have hoped that the law would have been clearer in one point, because there is an extension of the applicability in itself, in the issue of the so-called certificate. Where the foundation for electronic signature is created, a certificate is issued, and in my opinion, the law is too little and too little attention to safety. [[Congratulations at the FPÖ.]] I know that this was essentially taken over from the old law, but precisely because of the fact that one is expanding the applicability, I think one should have gone further. I'll explain briefly where my criticism is. In the extreme case you can get such a certificate without having to show or send an ID card and without being personally present at the exhibition or collection of the certificate. This is both possible. The text of the law reads: Either one presents an “official photo identification ” or one proves“ evidence equivalent to, documented or documented in its reliability. ” – So that’s already very bulky, you can’t imagine anything underneath it, and, frankly, that doesn’t replace the photo ID because you have to go back some steps or present any other document or show another proof. I do not understand why you do not simply and simply stick to the official photo identification and make a clear regulation. The second question is just this personal presence. It is also not necessary, as has been said. You can order and send this on the Internet. This combination is, of course, abuse-sensitive, and this is delicate, if you have no security at the time of issue of the certificate, because then everything that follows is of course not sufficiently safe. This is a real gap in the law, and it is a pity that the legislator, in which case the government parties, since did not take the step, say, if we extend the signature and if this is to be a future project, then we need to tighten up and improve the rules for issuing the certificate. Another point, besides other minors, is a lack of clarity and incomprehensibleness. These are, of course, detailed rules which are difficult to find here from the speaker's desk, but the writtenness itself is to be replaced with them. There are a few exceptions that are understandable. For example, last-will orders are only to be made in writing, but they are not here – so the can't be done with electronic signature – on the other hand, declarations of will of the family and inheritance law, which are bound to the form of writing or to a more stringent form, are systemarily made possible with the electronic signature. This is already in the law, but, as has already been said, even now, if one had already changed or redrafted a law, one could have made a different regulation, or one could also improve such things and bring in no system-absorbed regulations here. [[Congratulations at the FPÖ.]] This criticism and this – I would like to say so – slight disappointment that this opportunity to formulate it better and more precisely here has not been taken, leads us to reject this law.

ParlaMint-AT_2016-06-16-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00134_d7e7234	Madam President. Mrs. State Secretary! Ladies and gentlemen! I particularly welcome the new secondary schools from Uttendorf and Rum. [[General condolences.]] High house! First of all, I think that the implementation of a directive of the European Union is not just a choice, but also a duty that we have to fulfil. In this particular case, it concerns the eIDAS Directive, which is directly applicable. That is, many of these regulations, which you have criticised here, do not come from the law itself, but also from the wording – and the wording of directives, I give you the right, in particular the German translations, are not always very legal. However, this eIDAS Directive is an immediate application, that is to say, nothing other than the transition to uniform or specific standards existing in different states to ensure that the existing laws of signature, which have been established in different states, are harmonised or subject to certain standards, in order to ultimately, first of all, to define the conditions, including security, and international standards, in order to ensure that such an electronic signature is made in the individual Member States, that it has been verified, that it has been made according to conventional standards and can ultimately be used in accordance with conventional standards. This means, of course, an acceleration of the legal system, an acceleration of business – all of these possibilities. In itself, this development is to be welcomed because it is not sustainable and can also be implemented. Secondly, Those points which this directly applicable regulation does not regulate, of course, we can regulate nationally. The aim is now to lay down the rules on the form under which these certificates can be issued, as well as the rules on liability which such exhibitions bring with them, which also concerns supervision and control. This is what we regulate nationally in this signature and trust law, namely the supervision of these entire exhibition arrangements, the formal regulations and ultimately the liability regulations. And of course, a law, namely the § 886 of the ABGB, which has been in force since 1811, is no longer appropriate at a time when it is possible to deal with everything about electronic commerce, but that this must be taken into account in its own law, is actually a matter of course. As chairman of the Constitutional Committee, however, I would like to call on the authorities of the various ministries to redesign the legislative texts in this way, that they are also understandable. I have noticed that quite respectable lawyers, and I also count my previous speakers, including me, have had real difficulties with the understanding of this law, which is based on the legal language of the regulations of the European Union, to which you want to align yourself linguistically – and then that no longer works. I really appeal to everyone – to everyone! – Ministries to make the laws more understandable, to make the laws more understandable to the user, so that they do not need three lawyers to understand the meaning and purpose of a law. [[As a result, the Commission has not yet received any comments from interested parties.]] It would also be easier for our Members to make such a language uniform and effective. This is an appeal, as it has occurred more often in recent months, that the texts have been written in such a way that they are almost incomprehensible. In this sense, I still believe that this as a law is a good approach and a good implementation of the directive and will ultimately benefit the consumer.

When people print out the digital seal at home, of course, they have no signature on it, and this has often led to the fact that when you went abroad with this certificate – for example, I used such a one to marry – that people there said: That's not true, because there's no signature on it! These are the very foundations and the reasons why it is necessary to have a common European solution, a recognition of the different countries, so that, in the end, these things, which we have decided nationally, can continue to do so internationally. And with regard to the trust services that are now being offered – in the previous signature law, the Telekom Control Commission has carried out this task – this is now being taken over by the RTR, the Radio and Telekom Regulation GmbH, because it simply has the better conditions to include these trust services in a list, which makes it very easy for consumers to ask if these trust services are really these providers. As a consumer protection spokesperson, it is a very important requirement – and I am glad that this is now so recorded inside – that you can't hide anything, you can't have a hidden clause anymore, because it can't be that you're entering into a contract – in order to show it again by an example – which has already signed with the mobile signature, then maintain itself by e-mail and make changes – also with the mobile signature – but in the general terms and conditions there is somewhere that you can't get out with the mobile signature. This will not exist in the future, and this will protect consumers. That is why I think that digitization is the right step in the right direction.

ParlaMint-AT_2016-06-16-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00134_d7e7587	Madam President. Mrs. State Secretary! High house! I would just like to add the following request to Mr Wittmann's statement: [[Quoted print version removed]] In reality, only one word is changed, namely, the word “explanation” is replaced by the word “confirmation”. Why is that important? – This is important because the word “explanation” has been used in different meanings in this legal system: once in the sense of the law, once in the sense of the Notarial Order. Therefore, the word “confirmation” must be entered for clarification. However, there is also a second provision following colleagues Wittmann, which is something in my stomach, which is the matter with the clauses in the general terms and conditions. The Secretary of State has also said that hidden clauses are ineffective in general terms and conditions under certain circumstances. The interesting thing is that this prohibition of hidden clauses is only hidden in the law, namely in § 4 para. 3, where after two negations – and something like that, if you have two negations, it is difficult to read in the law too – there is the following condition: ‘... unless the trader proves that the contract provisions have been negotiated in detail’. You must find this provision in order to find the ban on hidden clauses. Indeed, there is nothing about clauses, but it says: “has been negotiated in detail. ” However, there are two things that the Legist has mixed: One is the material content that these provisions have been agreed, and the other is the obligation of proof. This provision is not at all accessible by the rules of logic, namely, as it says, the trader should first prove that these provisions are applicable, and then the consumer could only rely on whether or not to apply them. Of course, that can never be. It must be understood that this provision has been agreed accordingly and in the case of a trader the corresponding burden of proof must be assumed. These two things – the material content of this provision and the procedural burden of proof – we must: Hold each other. I hope that these statements for the first person who really needs to apply this will be clear that he knows what he really needs to do. By the way, of course, the provision “what has been negotiated in detail” is also a broad field for interpretations, since the terms and conditions are either applicable, void or obviously now have a third quality, namely that they are negotiated in detail. This can, of course, be a business condition if, for example, one highlights it boldly. Whether in the future people with the sentence: This contract as well as the terms and conditions have been negotiated in detail!, to sign, remains pending. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2016-06-16-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00134_d7e8005	Madam President. Mr President-in-Office of the Council. High house! Today, this debate is about money in terms of financial compensation. The report on the graded population key deals with the distribution of approximately €90 billion of federal funds to federal, regional and local authorities, and this distribution is not fair for the purposes of the municipalities because a graduated population key is applied. As an example of what flowers this can produce, I took along the “Tyrol daily” of October, which reports, among other things, that the city of Imst has now managed to count 10,042 inhabitants. That is to say, the population has exceeded the magical limit of 10,000. Why is that so important? – When you exceed this 10,000-inhabitant limit you get to a higher level and get more money per head. This means for the municipality of Imst over the period at least € 1 million in additional income in six years. That's God damn money. A side effect of this is: The salaries of mayors and vice-presidents also rise. There are also more councils and so on. This sometimes also leads to the fact that there are communities that are trying to attract people with the help of competitions so that they can skip certain classes. And that can't be it! It is just too scurry that a municipality with 10 040 inhabitants receives much more money – that is, € 1 million more over the period, over six years – than a municipality or a city that has 9 570 inhabitants. – That is unexplainable, unjust! This makes it possible to change the population key, which must be changed from the point of view of the municipalities. Every citizen must be equal in value, no matter where he lives, whether in the city or in the countryside. That's how you can always argue. This graded population key has arisen from the wars that have resulted in major destructions in the cities after which one has agreed to support the cities. That was understandable at the time, it's clear. But if you take the rural area now, it is also clear that there is an incredible task – keywords: Roads, roads, wastewater, canal – for which a lot of money is now needed, but which the communities do not have to the necessary extent. There are, therefore, these different stages that are to be abolished, namely, once to 10,000 inhabitants, then from 10,000 to 20,000, and then there is a leap of 20,000 to 50,000 and the level of over 50,000. For example, this is reflected in the fact that up to 10 000 inhabitants per capita receive €844 – as a result of a current request response – and in the span of 10 001 inhabitants up to €20 000 per capita. If you make it to over 50,000 inhabitants, as is the case in Innsbruck, then you get €1,334 per head. This is due to this graded population key, which is not fair and which must be replaced by the task orientation that the Court of Auditors has long called for. It would be high time to change this dissatisfying circumstance. But the signals that are broadcast are unfortunately others. It is a task-oriented to introduce that level – say: Federation, country or community – which fulfils the task, also receives the necessary money for it. But these current flows do not fit in in the back and front of the current financial compensation. An example of this is childcare. In child care, it is like this: The Federation and the countries create but the municipalities are responsible for carrying out the task. Only: The communities do not have the money to fulfill the wishes of federal and state governments. For example – and I want to tell you how I was a mayor – you can come up with the following wishes: longer opening hours, introduction of a lunch table, reduction of group sizes, maximum size 22, now 20, additional assistant staff, additional support staff, for example for each group of two people, once a person. It's worth everything! Only: The It is also to be financed! However, the communities do not have the necessary money, although they are responsible for it. The wishes are articulated by the federal government on the way across the countries. Only: The necessary money won't come! That is why I went to the competent councillor Palfrader and said to her: Mrs Landesratin, it would be great if we could get a grant for the expansion of the infrastructure once! For example, we want to buy new play equipment. Then came the lapidar answer – it was so! –: I'm not responsible! You, as a community, are responsible! - Well, I said, but that's great already: We should do everything, but we don't get the money for it! Accordingly, the budget of the municipalities will look forward to: 45 percent of the municipalities in Austria are no longer able to create a balanced budget. This is due to the downgraded population key by which the financial flows are so that the tasks to be fulfilled are not under-feducated with the necessary money. And that's to stop! In this direction, the Court of Auditors has worked very well. Our Court of Auditors will still thank you, Mr President, for the excellent work and for the many constructive proposals. Finally, I should like to thank you personally as former chairman of the Court of Auditors in the Tyrolean Landtag. There has always been excellent cooperation between the country of Tyrol and your institution, characterized by professional and academic competence and combined with many proposals. In Tyrol too, it is likely that those 20 percent of your proposals, which were not implemented, would have the strength that we would actually all need. In this sense I say thank you for your twelve-year work, and I wish you all the love and all the good. I hope that we will bring this financial compensation into line with a task orientation and that it will not end as the "Tyrol daily newspaper" entitled after the city day, namely that we are dealing with a "summit of money destruction". - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2016-06-16-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00134_d7e8486	Mr President. Mr President of the Court of Auditors! Dear ladies and gentlemen! The complexity of the financial compensation has already been explained by my previous speakers in many ways. I would now like to repeat two words, one on Mr Strolz's statement, which meant that when funds go to countries, they disappear into dark channels. – I reject this statement! They do not disappear into dark channels, but there is a task that the countries naturally fulfil. I am also saying that, and this applies both to the Federal Government and to the countries, as well as to the municipalities, which prove the Court of Auditors' reports, there are improvements, there are possibilities – and we must take account of them in our responsibility – to make optimum use of the financial resources available. - On the one hand. Secondly, I would like to support Mr Hauser's request, which meant that local authorities should not always distribute tasks to other local authorities without also saying how these tasks can be managed financially. I really want to support this demand, because such tasks meet us every day, especially at local level. [[Congratulation with the ÖVP and the Abg. Preiner.]] The Court of Auditors has produced a total of two reports on financial compensation, one with a particular focus on the key to the population and another with a focus on needs allocations. I can now make a few comments on the allocation of needs. On the one hand, the allocations of needs are deducted from the share of income for the municipality. These are a total of 12.7 percent, which then divide the countries among the communities with the aim of maintaining and restoring the balance of the household, meeting exceptional requirements – for example, school building, kindergarten construction, road renovations, construction of sports facilities and the like, if, in particular, smaller communities themselves do not have the financial possibilities to implement these projects – and of course also to ensure compensation for hardness. This objective, I have already mentioned, naturally means that smaller communities, in particular, benefit from these allocations of needs. These effects have also been demonstrated in the specific reviews carried out by the Federal States of Styria and Lower Austria. If one compares the financial power of the municipalities with the allocation of the needs of the municipalities without any need, it can be seen that, in particular, communities of less than 500 inhabitants have about that financial power at their disposal, such as the municipalities with over 10,000 inhabitants, when one breaks down this per capita. In summary, ladies and gentlemen, some aspects: On the one hand – and this has been mentioned several times – the financial compensation is, of course, a complication. and complex matter and from it often also an opaque matter. But as with many other materials, the complexity has developed from the past. It must also be said quite openly that every single measure that was put in place at a given time was correct and important at that time. Now we are faced with the challenge of simply implementing these tasks. Why is this so difficult? – Because there are always those affected, this also shows up in the ongoing financial settlement negotiations, because, of course, the total amount of money is no longer going to be and therefore there is really too great a problem in retransposition. For me personally as mayor, allocations of needs are a very important instrument for ensuring that, in particular, smaller communities can fulfil their tasks, that even smaller communities can create the infrastructure they need for their people. Ladies and gentlemen! Finally, I would like to thank you for these two reports. I can only recommend them to anyone who wants to deal more closely with this matter, the matter of financial compensation. They offer a very comprehensive and very transparent presentation of matter. Finally, I should like to join you in congratulating our Group spokesperson Hermann Gahran, Mr Moser, and wish you all the best.

ParlaMint-AT_2016-06-16-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00134_d7e8645	Mr President. Mr President Moser! High house! The report you have presented to us here, which focuses on two issues of financial compensation, is at the right time. The financial compensatory negotiations are under way, and they deal with two very serious issues: firstly, with the allocation of needs, and secondly with the permanent summit of financial compensation, the graded population key; secondly, at a permanent construction site, because that is the financial compensation for decades, I would say. And if I say that this has been a permanent construction site for decades, it already indicates that it has grown historically and thus also complex, opaque and, as you point out in your report, due to its complexity is also faulty. But not only that! They have massive criticism of this graded population key, and I do not think that is quite wrong. There are no clear objectives, as in the whole of the financial balance. There is no strategic framework, there are no criteria in the context of the graded population key for regional compensation. But there is also – and this is central, and I will come back to it – no orientation to the tasks, but the distribution of funds is essentially done at the population with a multiplier, which gives larger communities and cities more resources than smaller ones. This is controversial, but it has a certain margin, which the report also shows. Furthermore, you say that the financial compensation also preserves assets. This also applies to the overall financial compensation, and that is the essential point, which I repeatedly highlight: Unfortunately, the financial compensation – and even now in the ongoing financial compensatory negotiations – is only about the distribution of funds and the holding of assets in the entire tax cake. And because ownership is maintained, the graded population key has no place for changes, for example in the economic field, in the ecological field, nor does it have a place for demographic change. A growing city like Vienna, however, needs a very different treatment than for example shrinking regions. However, this is not expressed at all in the classified population key. There is also no incentive for community mergers, a subject which is certainly delicate, but which must not be excluded. I fully share this criticism, Mr President, which you are presenting here in your report, as well as your recommendations to the Minister for Finance. What must happen if we want to get rid of this acquis communautaire and think in old patterns and get a new financial balance from this permanent construction site, but also a new federalism model? Financial compensation and federalism must be thought in one thing, that is, almost Siamese twins. It's about overcoming the weaknesses you're addressing. This begins with the thinking of the acquis, it goes beyond the overcoming of the transfer chaos, which is due to the distribution of funds in such a way that one buys and the other pays, that is a very bad mixture, but also to the fact that we are orienting the distribution of funds not to multiplyers who are politically negotiated and are not oriented to objectives, but to the actual tasks to be fulfilled by countries and communities and by the federal government. However, it is also about the need for greater responsibility for the collection of funds for the countries and communities. Good governance has to take place, instead of bad governance, good governance has to take place. But the central question is a question that has been discussed for a long time: How do we get out of the dead end of federalism? Indeed, political scientists, as well as economists, are very skeptical about whether we can find the way out of this dead end. But one thing is central to this: It is not only about overcoming the weaknesses that have been shown, but also about re-establishing a process. There, I think you can learn a lot from Switzerland. There needs to be a new culture of dialogue within the financial compensation partner. It cannot be that, if one makes a proposal – the countries, for example – the federal government then says post-turning, no, if not – and vice versa. That means we have to get out of these possessions. We need to get out of these thinking patterns. We need to get away from the blockade, especially those of the princes, who are also being transported in the ongoing financial compensatory negotiations. Only then will we achieve a new financial compensation with a new federalism model. We are currently at a turn in Austria: Do we want to continue with 15a agreements, such as since the 1920s, or do we want to go to a constructive federalism? Negotiations are under way. The Minister of Finance is required. He started with a lot of words when he took office. We'll see what comes out at the end of the day. However, as a learned Austrian, who has been dealing with this topic for many decades, I must say: My optimism is limited. Thank you.

Why is the whole thing so complicated? – On the one hand, because, in reality, it is not only about the one financial compensation, but also about thousands of financial flows and payment flows in the secondary and tertiary financial compensation, because the complexity of these payments has increased in such a way that there are only a few experts who can also see the system and, on the other hand, because there are different situations of interest, namely, not only between the federal government, countries and municipalities – we have already discussed it here – but also countries against countries, large communities against smaller towns, against municipalities, cities and municipalities against countries. There are different expectations and views, which is perfectly logical. Mr Gamon has said it quite honestly, of course this is also a debate in the political groups, where it makes a difference whether one is active in local policy or takes responsibility in the country or in the federal government. It is all the more important – that is, I believe, also the central task of the Court of Auditors, since it also helps in this debate – that one really puts his finger on the wound, that one has looked at the construction sites in fact and that these are now also on the table in this hot phase. One is the complexity that we urgently need to reduce. It can't be that five out of eight of the federal audited states calculate and the municipalities the payment flows cannot even verify, because this is simply so complex and too opaque. Furthermore, the pooling of competence, no clear division of tasks and distribution of competence in financing. The area of kindergartens has been addressed, where the municipalities are in fact primarily responsible, there are nine national laws and the federal government may also co-finance, but in reality it is not possible to control and to demand genuinely sharp quality criteria. I believe that, in this area, we should urgently accept the Court of Auditors' recommendations that the money should also come to the cities and communities, where it should be understood that it is not redistributed by the countries and that it should be distributed again. Mr President, thank you very much for the recommendations, thank you and your team! I can personally wish you every good for your personal future.

ParlaMint-AT_2016-06-16-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00134_d7e9385	Mr President. High house! It is not very easy, after twelve years, to take the floor last time in this plenary session as President of the Court of Auditors. I would like to start by thanking all of you very much for the constant recognition of the work of the Court of Auditors, its employees. I would also like to thank you again and again for the appreciation expressed in personal discussions over the past 12 years. In particular, I would like to thank the members of the Committee on Budgets and, in particular, the Committee of the Regions, the Committee members and their alternates, as well as the leaders of the political groups for their excellent cooperation, for the discussion on matters relating to the Court of Auditors across party borders, while at the same time ensuring that you have brought a common awareness of the problem into this plenary session. It was a pleasure for me that you, ladies and gentlemen, over the past 12 years have always appreciated the work that the Court of Auditors' objective and independent work has had. The staff of the Court of Auditors have shown extraordinary commitment and contributed to the fact that, as you have often mentioned in the debate, the Court of Auditors has certainly become one of the leading institutions in Austria for citizens. In the last 12 years, the Court has submitted a total of 1 175 reports to the Federal Government, countries, municipalities, social security institutions and chambers, as well as 19 federal accounts, 12 income reports, 15 position papers and 11 performance reports. Over the years, the audit share in the Court of Auditors has increased from 68 percent to 80 percent. At the same time, recommendations from the Court of Auditors were implemented by 80 percent, with the Court of Auditors making a total of 16 171 recommendations from 2007 to 2015. This is an internationally recognised implementation value, which has also been possible only by the fact that the Court of Auditors has given the highest attention to quality and at the same time has taken measures to increase its effectiveness. 60 projects were implemented in the Court of Auditors by employees. We have run a qualification and quality offensive, and we have also been tested. We have thus also answered the question of who checks the auditors. The German Court of Auditors, the Swiss Court of Auditors and the Danish Court of Auditors have examined the Austrian Court of Auditors closely. As a result of the examination, they have noted in their audit report that the Austrian Court of Auditors, with its technical requirements and organisational steps, has made exemplary progress in improving the quality of the report. A large number of lectures, press conferences and interviews have led to 76 000 reports dealing with the issue of the Court of Auditors in the years 2004 to 2016. We have also launched an effective offensive by initiating follow-up checks and a demand process. All this has led to an implementation rate of 80 percent. This means that 80 percent of the recommendations have been or will be implemented. This was only possible – and I am well aware of this – because you, ladies and gentlemen, have always appreciated the work of the Court of Auditors, what you have done in the debate today, and have taken advantage of your opportunities to implement the Court of Auditors' recommendations. You, ladies and gentlemen, have the opportunity – you have the authority of control, you have the budgetary authority – to implement measures in fact, while the Court of Auditors has the task of examining and consulting and providing you with the basis for them. I therefore urge you, with all the emphasis, to tackle the remaining 20 percent of the recommendations – this was also addressed today – especially in the areas of education, health, care, social affairs, promotions or deregulations, and in particular the streamlining of financial relations between the Federal Government, countries and communities, which can only ensure sustainable performance of tasks. The Court of Auditors, in its capacity as General Secretariat of INTOSAI, has also done exceptional things internationally. Three UN General Assembly resolutions to promote efficient, accountable, effective, transparent public administration have been reached by strengthening audit courts. Three UN Economic and Social Council resolutions have also been reached, as well as an explicit commitment to strengthening the accounting courts in the Addis-Abeba Declaration on the United Nations Agenda 2030. These successes were only possible because 192 billing courts could be won to support these initiatives, and because the Federal Ministry of Foreign Affairs and the Permanent Representation of Austria to the United Nations supported the General Secretariat in this regard. These are successes that can be seen nationally and internationally and which were also recognized by 192 members of INTOSAI by the award of the Jörg Kandusch Award. This award has been awarded because the Court of Auditors has provided special merits and services for our international reform programme and the achievement of the resolutions. These successes have made possible the staff of the Court of Auditors over the last 12 years. I am proud that I have been able to lead the Court of Auditors' team over the past twelve years, and I would like to thank all of my heart to my employees, who have been working in the service of citizens with extraordinary commitment and incessant commitment to greater transparency and accountability. In this very positive balance sheet, however, it should not be forgotten that there is still a great deal of need for action. 20 percent of the recommendations have not been implemented, especially in those areas where the interaction of local authorities where systems change and skills change are required. There is the need to eliminate division of competence, simplify decision-making structures and strengthen transparency and accountability so that public services can actually reach those concerned in the future. That this is necessary is demonstrated by the six reports on the agenda today, in particular the two reports addressed in connection with the graded population key in the financial balance and the payments flows between the local authorities. These examinations have shown that funds between the federal, state and local authorities are in transparent, difficult to understand and, at the same time, are allocated as a priority for the preservation of possessions. Current developments – which have already been addressed –, demographic changes, changes in expenditure are not sufficiently taken into account as well as a financial balance, regional compensation or task orientation. The highly complex regulations lead to the miscalculation of the distribution of public funds by the countries and, in many cases, misbooked by the municipalities. There is a need for action! There is a need for action in many areas of public administration in order to ensure the sustainability of tasks for citizens. This was expressed by the Court of Auditors in 2011 with its 599 recommendations. 50 percent of these recommendations have been implemented so far. Since then, the Court of Auditors has published 373 reports, and these reports show that further development must take place in all areas of public administration in order to ensure the sustainability of tasks. The Court of Auditors shall have the task of examining and consulting. For this reason, we have now presented a position paper for sustainable development in Austria with 1,007 recommendations from 23 fields of action to ensure the sustainable provision of public services in Austria. I'm looking for you, my dear. Ladies and gentlemen, to take advantage of your parliamentary possibilities and to push ahead with the implementation of the recommendations, because more transparency, more accountability, a greater responsibility for results makes Austria stronger. For this, the Court of Auditors has always been and is a reliable, independent, objective and effective partner. I am pleased that I can hand over the Court of Auditors as the best practice for excellence in testing and consulting. I am also pleased that my successor will be handed over a nationally and internationally recognised Court of Auditors and, at the same time, deposits of €5 million. At this point, I would like to thank the staff of the Court of Auditors once again for their excellent work, their support and their tireless commitment. I am sure that my successor will also carry out her task with full commitment, wishing her to get through with the figures and facts and get her full support. The figures and facts show that it is necessary to finally let the will follow. As Nelson Mandela so appropriate said: Yesterday is history, tomorrow and opportunity, today is all we have. Let’s build together now. My twelve years as President of the Court of Auditors are now history. Today's election of the new President of the Court of Auditors will open a new chapter of control in Austria. The benefit will be to make the morning a chance with our actions! Thank you very much.

Lugar]] But this is also linked to a kind of financial autonomy, which we certainly need to discuss even longer. To this end, an educational foundation will be set up which will bring a competition for financial resources for innovative projects to schools. There were not many additional suggestions in the legislative process, but some proposals have been taken into account. That is why I am also bringing forward the amendment tabled by the honourable Members. Elisabeth Grossmann, Brigitte Jank, colleagues on the draft law in the report of the Teaching Committee on the Government Template 1146 of the Supplements to the School Amendment Act 2016, 1167 of the Supplements. In view of the extent of this request, we have the Madam President in accordance with § 53 para. 4 Mr Gog asked to have the distribution to the Members, and you had this amendment in mind. I just want to go into it very briefly. What is the point of this amendment? – In the course of the review, letters have been sent to us, which indicate that the current arrangements for the compulsory implementation of advanced sub-examinations to AHS and BMHS do not take into account the differences in the individual schools to the extent necessary. With this amendment the decision will be taken autonomously at the respective school location by the school management after consultation of the School Community Committee, i.e. a further step towards autonomy. The determination of final audits will be postponed by one year both to the BMHS and to the AHS and will only be applied with the main date from 2018 onwards. Therefore, early in the 2017/18 school year, preferential sub-examinations with respect to the main date of 2018 may be made mandatory if the subject-matter corresponding to the subject-matter of the examination has been completed and completed positively by the school year 2016/17. The current legal situation shall continue to apply to the implementation of preferential sub-examinations at the beginning of the school year 2016/17, the main date of 2017. The decision at the school location will cover all classes of a year. The separation between BHS and BMS as well as the construction courses at such locations is clear. After the bill has been signed, the next step will be to adapt the examination rules so that preparation for the preferred oral partial examinations is possible. Thank you.

There's a silent assimilation. This trend has to be stopped, and in particular the language has central importance for the population population population. The language is the means of communication for the ethnic group members. If it is no longer spoken, the ethnic group ceases to exist. For this reason, education and education is of central importance for the preservation of a people's group. Here, as it were, the foundation is laid for the children to learn from kindergarten to the national language groups and thus also to pass on the popular culture. In autumn, the educational reform was presented. The aim of the education reform is to improve and develop the quality of education and school life. Of course, the outcome of the educational reform was of interest to the Austrian ethnic groups; they also asked for more often in the Ministry of Education. Alone, it's said they didn't get any information. I then put a parliamentary question, and then suddenly negotiations came, which is also right, because, as has been said, this is of central importance. It was always the concern of the Austrian ethnic groups that there was a bilingual support and also training from kindergarten to maturity examination, that you can be taught and further trained throughout the German language, but also in the ethnic groups languages. Therefore, it is of central importance for the groups of peoples as regards kindergartens and preschools, how the educational offer looks like quality assurance. And it's also about pedagogical supervision. Therefore, some things are contained in the question, whose answer now exists. There are no changes in the content of minority education, the legal provisions of the two minority school laws for Burgenland and Carinthia are not affected. The ethnic groups need not fear that traditional multilingualism is also at risk in view of the current refugee discussion, which was a concern of the peoples – but that is too little. It cannot be that it is enough to say that there is nothing going on in the minority school sector, because the population population is at risk, as has been said. Therefore, we also need a further development, not only in the autochthonic settlement area, but also in the settlement area outside the autochthonic area, such as in Vienna. There are a number of people's needs, and I hope that a new professional situation, family situation in education reform, will also be taken into account with the aim of securing the existence of the Austrian ethnic groups. Thank you.
